From KP-I Lump Solution to Travelling wave of 3D Gravity Capillary Water wave problem
Changfeng Gui, Shanfa Lai, Yong Liu, Juncheng Wei, Wen Yang

Abstract
. In this paper, we study the three-dimensional gravity-capillary water wave problem involving an irrotational, perfect fluid with gravity and surface tension. We focus on steady waves propagating uniformly in one direction. Assuming constant wave speed and water depth, we analyze the fluid’s velocity potential and boundary conditions. Using the Kadomtsev-Petviashvili (KP)-I equation as a simplified model, we show that, within a specific parameter range, the problem admits a fully localized solitary-wave solution resemble lump type solutions of the KP-I equation.



1.
Introduction

This article explores the classical three-dimensional gravity-capillary water-wave problem, which models the irrotational flow of an inviscid fluid with constant density, subject to gravity and surface tension forces. In the 1750s, L. Euler introduced the first comprehensive mathematical model for fluid dynamics, a framework that remains pivotal in coastal engineering, naval architecture, and environmental science. For an incompressible fluid, the velocity field at a point
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and time
t
is denoted by
\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x},t)=(u_{1}(\mathbf{x},t),u_{2}(\mathbf{x},t),u_{3}(\mathbf{x},t))
. The Euler equations, which govern the motion of an inviscid fluid, are expressed as:

(1.1)

\frac{\partial\mathbf{u}}{\partial t}+(\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{u}+\nabla P=\mathbf{F},

where
P(\mathbf{x},t)
is the pressure, and
\mathbf{F}
represents external forces, such as gravity, acting on the fluid. The fluid domain is typically bounded below by a rigid, horizontal bottom at a fixed depth and above by a free surface, described by the function
\eta(x_{1},x_{2},t)
. The problem is formulated as follows:

(1.2)

\begin{cases}\frac{\partial\mathbf{u}}{\partial t}+(\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{u}+\nabla P=\mathbf{F}&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\nabla\cdot\mathbf{u}=0&\text{in }\Omega,\end{cases}

where the domain is defined as:

\Omega:=\left\{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:-d<x_{3}<\eta(x_{1},x_{2},t)\right\}.

Assuming the flow is irrotational, the convective term in the Euler equations simplifies to
(\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{u}=\frac{1}{2}\nabla|\mathbf{u}|^{2}
. For gravity-capillary waves, the external force is given by
\mathbf{F}=g\mathbf{e}_{3}
, where
g
represents the gravitational acceleration. Consequently, the momentum equation in (
1.2
) can be rewritten as:

\frac{\partial\mathbf{u}}{\partial t}+\nabla\left(\frac{1}{2}|\mathbf{u}|^{2}+P+gx_{3}\right)=0.

On the free surface
x_{3}=\eta({\bf x}^{\prime},t)
with
{\bf x}^{\prime}=(x_{1},x_{2})
, the kinematic boundary condition is:

(1.3)

\partial_{t}\eta=\sqrt{1+(\partial_{x_{1}}\eta)^{2}+(\partial_{x_{2}}\eta)^{2}}\,\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{n},

which reflects that the free surface evolves with the motion of the fluid. At the bottom boundary, the impermeability condition is:

(1.4)

\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{n}=0,

where
\mathbf{n}
is the unit outward normal to the boundary of the fluid domain in (
1.3
) and (
1.4
). The irrotationality condition implies that the velocity field is given by
\mathbf{u}=\nabla\Phi
, where
\Phi(\mathbf{x},t)
is the velocity potential. In terms of
\Phi
, the system (
1.2
) can be expressed as:

(1.5)

\begin{cases}\nabla\left(\partial_{t}\Phi+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\Phi|^{2}+P+gx_{3}\right)=0&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\Delta\Phi=0&\text{in }\Omega,\end{cases}

where the second equation arises from the incompressibility condition. The first equation in (
1.5
) implies:

(1.6)

\partial_{t}\Phi+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\Phi|^{2}+P+gx_{3}=C(t),

where
C(t)
is a constant depending only on time. Evaluating (
1.6
) at the free surface
x_{3}=\eta(x_{1},x_{2},t)
, we obtain the dynamic boundary condition, known as Bernoulli’s principle:

(1.7)

\partial_{t}\Phi+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\Phi|^{2}+P+g\eta=0.

When the pressure at the free surface is determined by surface tension, it is given by:

P=\sigma\operatorname{div}\left(\frac{\nabla_{\perp}\eta}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla_{\perp}\eta|^{2}}}\right),

where
\nabla_{\perp}=(\partial_{x_{1}},\partial_{x_{2}})
and
\sigma
is the coefficient of the surface tension. Combining these, the boundary value problem for the gravity-capillary water-wave system is:

(1.8)

\begin{cases}\Delta\Phi=0&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{n}\Phi=0&\text{on }x_{3}=-d,\\
\partial_{t}\eta=\partial_{x_{3}}\Phi-\nabla_{\perp}\eta\cdot\nabla_{\perp}\Phi&\text{on }x_{3}=\eta(x_{1},x_{2},t),\\
\partial_{t}\Phi=-\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\Phi|^{2}-g\eta+\sigma\operatorname{div}\left(\frac{\nabla_{\perp}\eta}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla_{\perp}\eta|^{2}}}\right)&\text{on }x_{3}=\eta(x_{1},x_{2},t).\end{cases}

An extensive body of research addresses the water wave problem, investigating topics such as well-posedness in the presence of vorticity, solitary wave and front-type solutions, the geometry and properties of the interface, wave regularity, stagnation points and critical layers of the wave, stability and instability, and other related areas. Given the extensive scope of the literature, a complete survey of all contributions is infeasible; we therefore point to a selection of works and refer readers to
[
2
,
3
,
4
,
7
,
8
,
9
,

,
11
,

,

,
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
,
18
,
19
,

,
22
,
23
,
24
,
25
,
26
,
29
,
32
,
34
,
41
,
42
,
43
,
44
,
45
,
51
,
52
,
53
,
54
,
55
,
56
,
60
,
61
,

67
,
68
,
69
,

,

,

,

,
74
,
75
,
76
]
and the references therein for further details.

The primary focus of this paper is on traveling wave solutions. For an in-depth review of recent developments in traveling water waves, we direct readers to
[
40
]
. Our investigation concentrates on the 3D water wave problem, where the three-dimensional setting introduces a variety of geometric configurations, with the free surface displaying distinct behaviors in different horizontal directions. Two classes of traveling waves have attracted significant attention: doubly periodic waves, which are periodic in two independent horizontal directions, and fully localized waves, which decay in all horizontal directions. The doubly periodic case, often referred to as short-crested waves, was first rigorously analyzed by Reeder-Shinbrot
[
59
]
using the Crandall-Rabinowitz bifurcation theorem in a fundamental domain of the “symmetric diamond” type, where the lattice directions have equal length. Earlier formal computations for such waves were provided by Fuchs
[
28
]
and Sretenskiĭ
[
64
]
. Subsequently, Craig-Nicholls
[
20
]
extended the analysis to arbitrary fundamental domains using a variational approach. Specifically, doubly periodic traveling waves are critical points of the energy functional

(1.9)

E(\eta,\Phi)=\int_{\Omega_{0}}\left(\int_{-d}^{\eta}\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\Phi|^{2}dx_{3}+\frac{1}{2}g\eta^{2}+\sigma(\sqrt{1+|\nabla_{\perp}\eta|^{2}}-1)\right)dx_{1}dx_{2},

subject to the constraints

(1.10)

I_{j}(\eta,\Phi)=\int_{\Omega_{0}}\eta_{x_{j}}\Phi(x_{1},x_{2},\eta(x_{1},x_{2}))dx_{1}dx_{2}={\rm const},~j=1,2,

where
\Omega_{0}
denotes the fundamental domain of the lattice,
\eta
is the free surface elevation,
\Phi
is the velocity potential, (g) is the gravitational acceleration, and
\sigma
is the surface tension coefficient. Since the velocity potential
\Phi
is harmonic, the problem can be reformulated using the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator (DNO) to reduce the dimensionality. Define the surface velocity potential as

\xi(x_{1},x_{2},t)=\Phi(x_{1},x_{2},\eta(x_{1},x_{2},t),t).

The DNO, denoted
G(\eta)
, is defined by

G(\eta)\xi=\partial_{x_{3}}\Phi-\nabla_{\perp}\eta\cdot\nabla_{\perp}\Phi,

where
\nabla_{\perp}=(\partial_{x_{1}},\partial_{x_{2}})
and the normal derivative is evaluated at the free surface. Craig and Groves
[
17
]
employed the DNO to derive long-wave models, with further developments in
[
5
,
21
,
18
]
. For sufficiently small
C^{1}
-norm of
\eta
, the DNO is analytic and admits the expansion

(1.11)

G(\eta)=G_{0}+G_{1}(\eta)+G_{2}(\eta)+R_{3}(\eta),

where the leading terms are

(1.12)

\displaystyle G_{0}=|D|\tanh(|D|),

\displaystyle G_{1}(\eta)=-G_{0}\eta G_{0}-D\eta\cdot D,

\displaystyle G_{2}(\eta)=-\frac{1}{2}|D|^{2}\eta^{2}G_{0}-\frac{1}{2}G_{0}\eta^{2}|D|^{2}+G_{0}\eta G_{0}\eta G_{0},

with
D=-i\nabla_{\perp}
and
|D|=\sqrt{-\Delta_{\perp}}
. Using the DNO, the kinematic and dynamic boundary conditions of the water wave problem (
1.8
) reduce to

(1.13)

\begin{cases}\eta_{t}=G(\eta)\xi,\\
\xi_{t}=\frac{1}{2(1+|\nabla_{\perp}\eta|^{2})}\left[(G(\eta)\xi+\nabla_{\perp}\eta\cdot\nabla_{\perp}\xi)^{2}-|\nabla_{\perp}\xi|^{2}|\nabla_{\perp}\eta|^{2}-|\nabla_{\perp}\xi|^{2}\right]\\
\quad\quad-g\eta+\sigma\operatorname{div}\left(\frac{\nabla_{\perp}\eta}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla_{\perp}\eta|^{2}}}\right).\end{cases}

The energy functional (
1.9
) can then be rewritten as

(1.14)

\int_{\Omega_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\xi G(\eta)\xi+\frac{1}{2}g\eta^{2}+\sigma(\sqrt{1+|\nabla_{\perp}\eta|^{2}}-1)\right)dx_{1}dx_{2}.

The existence of doubly periodic waves has been demonstrated using spatial dynamics techniques. Groves and Mielke
[
36
]
confirmed their existence in the symmetric diamond configuration, while Groves and Haragus
[
35
]
and Nilsson
[
57
]
generalized these findings to arbitrary fundamental domains. Advances in the study of doubly periodic water waves with vorticity have also emerged. Lokharu, Seth, and Wahlén
[
49
]
provided foundational evidence for small-amplitude waves in this setting. Later, Groves et al.
[
37
]
investigated three-dimensional waves on Beltrami flows through a generalized Dirichlet-Neumann operator. Most recently, Seth, Varholm, and Wahlén
[
63
]
furthered these findings by confirming the existence of symmetric gravity-capillary waves with small vorticity, achieving a key breakthrough in the field.

For fully localized 3D solitary waves, where the free surface
\eta(x_{1},x_{2})\to 0
as
|(x_{1},x_{2})|\to\infty
, spatial dynamics methods, successful in 2D, are less effective in 3D due to the presence of two unbounded variables. Instead, two major approaches are employed: variational methods and the implicit function theorem. As the periodic case, the Fully localised solitary waves are critical points of the energy functional

\displaystyle E(\eta,\xi)=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\xi G(\eta)\xi+\frac{1}{2}g\eta^{2}+\sigma(\sqrt{1+|\nabla\eta|^{2}}-1)\right)d{\bf x}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\phi|^{2}d{\bf x}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}g\eta^{2}+\sigma(\sqrt{1+|\nabla\eta|^{2}}-1)\right)d{\bf x}^{\prime},

subject to the momentum constraint in the
x_{1}
-direction,

I_{1}(\eta,\xi):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\eta_{x}\xi d{\bf x}^{\prime}={\rm const}.

Groves-Sun constructed solutions using a mountain-pass approach for the augmented energy
E_{c}(\eta,\xi)=E(\eta,\xi)-cI_{1}(\eta,\xi),
under the conditions
\frac{\sigma}{gd^{2}}>\frac{1}{3}
and wave speed
c
close to
\sqrt{gd}
. Buffoni-Groves-Sun-Wahlén
[
9
]
employed a variational method minimizing
E(\eta,\xi)
subject to a small momentum constraint, with further results for weak surface tension in
[
10
]
.

The implicit function theorem offers an alternative approach for 3D solitary waves. The water wave problem is closely related to model equations such as the Davey-Stewartson system and the Kadomtsev-Petviashvili (KP) equation. For the gravity-capillary problem on infinite depth, the Davey-Stewartson system reduces to a 2D elliptic nonlinear Schrödinger equation, enabling Buffoni, Groves, and Wahlén
[
11
]
to construct traveling wave solutions via elegant perturbation arguments. The connection between the KP equation and the water wave problem is explored in Ablowitz-Segur
[
1
]
, with additional details available in
[
17
, Section 6]
and
[
56
]
. To clarify this relationship, we consider the linearized boundary conditions

\begin{cases}\eta_{t}=\Phi_{x_{3}},\\
\Phi_{t}=-g\eta+\sigma\Delta\eta.\end{cases}

at the free surface. Solving these with the Laplace equation and bottom boundary condition yields the dispersion relation

\omega^{2}=\left(g\kappa+\sigma\kappa^{3}\right)\tanh\left(\kappa d\right).

where
\kappa=\sqrt{k^{2}+l^{2}}
is the wave number. For shallow water (
\kappa h\ll 1
) and weak transverse variation (
\frac{l}{k}\ll 1
), the dispersion relation simplifies to

\omega^{2}\approx gd\kappa^{2}\left(1+\frac{\sigma}{g}\kappa^{2}\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{3}(\kappa d)^{2}\right).

Assuming
l^{2}=O(k^{4})
, the dispersion relation yields the linear part of the KP equation:

\frac{1}{c_{0}}\eta_{tx}+\eta_{xx}+\frac{1}{2}\eta_{yy}+\frac{d^{2}}{6}\left(1-\frac{3\sigma}{gd^{2}}\right)\eta_{xxxx}=0,

where
c_{0}=\sqrt{gd}
. For
\frac{\sigma}{gd^{2}}>\frac{1}{3}
, this corresponds to the KP-I equation, supporting small-amplitude solitary waves. In fact, there is an explicit family of solutions with algebraic decay, first found by Manakov et al.
[
50
]
. The lump solutions have been shown to be nondegenerate and orbital stability by Liu-Wei
[
46
]
. While for
\frac{\sigma}{gd^{2}}<\frac{1}{3}
, it is KP-II, where such solutions are not expected. Further discussions are found in Lannes
[
44
]
and numerical studies by Părău, Vanden-Broeck and Cooker
[
58
]
. Notably, in the context of the 2D water wave problem, the Korteweg-de Vries (KdV) equation, rather than the KP-I equation, is typically employed as an approximating model. For traveling wave solutions, Groves
[
33
]
utilized solutions to the stationary KdV equation to establish the existence of solutions for the 2D water wave problem when the surface tension parameter satisfies
\frac{\sigma}{gd^{2}}>\frac{1}{3}
. In this paper, we aim to provide a definitive resolution for the 3D water wave problem.

To streamline our analysis, we take the following normalization:

(1.15)

\mbox{Gravitational acceleration constant}~\ g=1\mbox{ and the water depth}~\ d=1.

Under these assumptions, the corresponding KP-I equation takes the elliptic form

(1.16)

\left(\sigma-\frac{1}{3}\right)\partial_{x}^{4}q-\partial_{x}^{2}q-\partial_{y}^{2}q-\frac{3}{2}\partial_{x}((\partial_{x}q)^{2})=0,\quad\sigma>\frac{1}{3}.

In
[
46
]
, Liu-Wei proved that the lump solution of the equation (
1.16
) is non-degenerated. Based on this finding, we aim to establish

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
q(x,y)
be a non-degenerate lump solution to the KP-I equation (
1.16
). For any sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a solution pair
(\eta_{\varepsilon},\xi_{\varepsilon})
to the water wave problem (
1.8
) with traveling speed
c=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\varepsilon^{2}}}
, satisfying the asymptotic expansions:

\displaystyle\eta_{\varepsilon}(x_{1},x_{2},t)=\varepsilon^{2}Q(\varepsilon(x_{1}-ct),\varepsilon^{2}x_{2})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{3}),

\displaystyle\xi_{\varepsilon}(x_{1},x_{2},t)=\varepsilon q(\varepsilon(x_{1}-ct),\varepsilon^{2}x_{2})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),

where
Q(x,y):=\partial_{x}q(x,y)
denotes the spatial derivative of the lump profile.

In the following, we briefly outline the main steps of this article. Initially, we employ the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator to transform the full problem (
1.8
) into (
1.13
). We seek solutions to (
1.13
) of the form

\eta(x_{1},x_{2},t)=\varepsilon^{2}h(\varepsilon(x_{1}-ct),\varepsilon^{2}x_{2}),~\xi(x_{1},x_{2},t)=\varepsilon f(\varepsilon(x_{1}-ct),\varepsilon^{2}x_{2}),~c=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\varepsilon^{2}}}.

Substituting this ansatz into (
1.13
), we find that the leading-order term of the compatibility condition links the problem to the KP-I equation, which is similar to the traveling wave solutions of the Gross-Pitaevskii equation in
[
47
]
. However, as
[
47
]
, constructing solutions via the non-degeneracy of the lump solution and the classical Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction is not easy. Two challenges arise: first, perturbation terms complicate the decay properties and the nonlinear problem; second, the presence of the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator (DNO) requires careful analysis of its leading-order term. Instead of handling a constant anisotropic differential operator as in the Gross-Pitaevskii equation, we address a more complicated operator:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}=

\displaystyle\,A\partial_{1}^{4}-\partial_{1}^{2}-(1+\varepsilon^{2})\partial_{2}^{2}+\left(2A+\frac{1}{3}\right)\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{1}^{2}\partial_{2}^{2}+\sigma(1+\varepsilon^{2})\varepsilon^{4}\partial_{2}^{4}

(1.17)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{-4}(1+\varepsilon^{2})(1+\varepsilon^{2}\sigma\mathcal{P})(\mathcal{Q}G_{0}-\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{P})-\frac{3}{c}\partial_{1}(\partial_{1}q\partial(\cdot)),

where

(1.18)

A=(1+\varepsilon^{2})\sigma-\frac{1}{3},\quad\mathcal{P}=-\partial_{1}^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{2}^{2},\quad\mathcal{Q}=1+\frac{1}{3}\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{P},\quad G_{0}=|D|\tanh(|D|).

The term involving
G_{0}=|D|\tanh(|D|)
poses significant challenges in studying the linearized problem. A Taylor expansion is typically used, but to preserve the elliptic nature of the differential operator in Proposition
4.1
, we restrict the surface tension constant
\sigma
to a narrower range. Instead, we utilize the continued fraction representation of the hyperbolic tangent:

(1.19)

\tanh(x)=\cfrac{1}{\frac{1}{x}+\cfrac{1}{\frac{3}{x}+\cfrac{1}{\frac{5}{x}+\cfrac{1}{\frac{7}{x}+\ddots}}}},

which yields the inequality

(1.20)

\frac{x(15+x^{2})}{15+6x^{2}}>\tanh x>\frac{10x^{3}+105x}{x^{4}+45x^{2}+105}.

This inequality aids the analysis of the linearized operator in Proposition
4.1
. Having developed the
L^{2}
-theory for the linearized problem, we analyze the linear problem in a suitable Sobolev space to tackle the nonlinear problem. In
[
47
]
, a crucial step for the traveling wave solutions of the Gross-Pitaevskii equation involves deriving the asymptotic behavior of the Green function for the linearized operator at infinity and near zero. However, these techniques are not directly applicable here. Instead, we apply the classical Fourier analysis results, including the Hausdorff-Young inequality and the Hardy-Littlewood-Paley theorem, to complete the linear theory within certain Sobolev spaces. Subsequently, we address the nonlinear system by proving that it is weakly decoupled, which allows us to reduce it into a single nonlinear problem. This problem includes several nonlocal operators stemming from the DNO operator. To handle these terms, we utilize results from Craig-Schanz-Sulem
[
21
]
regarding two types of singular integral operators:

\displaystyle S_{l}(\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{l})\xi({\bf x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K({\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime})\left(\prod_{j=1}^{l}P(\eta_{j})\right)\xi({\bf y}^{\prime})\,d{\bf y}^{\prime},

\displaystyle S_{l,*}(\eta,\dots,\eta)\xi({\bf x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K_{*}({\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime})\kappa_{*}^{r}Q^{l}(\eta)\xi({\bf y}^{\prime})\,d{\bf y}^{\prime},

where
K
is a Calderón-Zygmund kernel satisfying standard estimates, and the functions
P(\eta)
,
Q(\eta)
,
\kappa_{*}({\bf x}^{\prime},{\bf y}^{\prime})
, and
K_{*}
are defined as:

P(\eta)=\frac{\eta({\bf x}^{\prime})-\eta({\bf y}^{\prime})}{|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}|},\quad Q(\eta)=\frac{\eta({\bf x}^{\prime})+\eta({\bf y}^{\prime})}{(|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{\frac{1}{2}}},\quad\kappa_{*}({\bf x}^{\prime},{\bf y}^{\prime})=\frac{4}{(|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{\frac{1}{2}}},

K_{*}({\bf x}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{(|{\bf x}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{\rho/2}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{2}\left(\frac{x_{\ell}}{(|{\bf x}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right)^{m_{\ell}}~\text{for }l+\rho+r>2~\mbox{and}~m_{\ell}\geq 0,~\ell=1,2.

Using Lemma
3.1
and Lemma
3.2
, we obtain refined estimates for the differential operators
A(\eta)
and
B(\eta)
, which appear in the DNO as:

(1-B(\eta))G(\eta)\cdot=|D|\tanh(|D|)\cdot+A(\eta)\cdot.

With these results, we can manage the nonlinear perturbations and subsequently use a Banach fixed-point argument to establish the existence of solutions to (
1.13
).

We conclude the introduction with the following comments. First, the non-degeneracy of the lump solution
q
to equation (
1.16
) and its Morse index of 1 are critical for analyzing the linearized operator. Using this non-degeneracy, we can address the linear problem in a functional space with appropriate symmetry, where the linearized operator is invertible. We strongly believe that the methods developed in this article can be extended to excited solutions of the KP-I equation. Recent work in
[
48
]
has established the uniqueness of even solutions to the KP-I equation. If one can prove that any excited solution is non-degenerate and such solution has finite Morse index, then we can apply a similar argument as in Section 4 to develop the linear theory for the corresponding linearized operator. This could lead to the discovery of additional traveling wave solutions to the water wave problem. Second, in analyzing the linear theory, we must consider the nonlocal operator
G(\eta)
. Employing the framework from Craig-Schanz-Sulem
[
21
]
, we obtain a priori estimates for the linearized operator of the KP-I lump solution, carrying the nonlocal term. This analysis holds independent interest. We suspect that Littlewood-Paley theory could provide pointwise estimates for the associated Green kernel and its derivatives, facilitating a more straightforward approach to studying the linear problem. We intend to pursue this in future research. Finally, compared to findings in
[
9
,
38
]
, our method offers a more precise characterization of the asymptotic behavior of solutions as the traveling speed approaches the critical value. This could yield deeper insights into the spectrum, uniqueness, and dynamical properties of traveling wave solutions, which are valuable for further exploration.

The article is organized as follows: In Section 2, we reformulate the problem (
1.13
) and establish its connection to the KP-I equation. In Section 3, we analyze the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator and obtain refined estimates for related operators. In Section 4, we investigate the linear problem by studying the linearized KP-I operator with a nonlocal term around the lump solution. In Section 5, we solve the nonlinear problem using the contraction mapping principle, completing the proof of Theorem
1.1
. Some technical tools from Fourier analysis are provided in the Appendix.

Notations

The following mathematical notations and conventions are adopted throughout this paper:

•

Spatial coordinates
:
The three-dimensional position vector is denoted by
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
, where
x^{\prime}=(x_{1},x_{2})
represents the horizontal coordinates in the Cartesian plane. The scaled coordinates are defined as:

\displaystyle x=\varepsilon x_{1},\quad y=\varepsilon^{2}x_{2}

where
\varepsilon>0
is a dimensionless scaling parameter.

•

Differential operators
:

\displaystyle\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=(\partial_{x_{1}},\partial_{x_{2}})

(horizontal gradient)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=(\partial_{x},\varepsilon\partial_{y})

(scaled gradient operator)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=(-i\partial_{x_{1}},-i\partial_{x_{2}})

(Fourier multiplier operator)

Here
\partial_{x}\equiv\partial_{1}
and
\partial_{y}\equiv\partial_{2}
denote partial derivatives with respect to the scaled coordinates
x
and
y
respectively. The magnitude satisfies
|D|=(D\cdot D)^{1/2}=(-\Delta_{{\bf x^{\prime}}})^{1/2}
, where
\Delta_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}=\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}+\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}
is the horizontal Laplacian.

•

Function spaces
:

\displaystyle\|\cdot\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2};{\bf x^{\prime}})}

\displaystyle:=\underset{{\bf x^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}{\mathrm{ess\,sup}}\,|\cdot|

(supremum norm)

\displaystyle\|\cdot\|_{W^{k,p}(\mathbb{R}^{2};{\bf x^{\prime}})}

\displaystyle:=\left(\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\|D^{\alpha}\cdot\|^{p}_{L^{p}}\right)^{1/p}

(Sobolev norm)

where
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is the order of weak derivatives,
1\leq p\leq\infty
is the integrability exponent, and differentiation is with respect to
{\bf x^{\prime}}
coordinates. The notation
\|\cdot\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
and
\|\cdot\|_{W^{k,p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
implicitly refers to the standard Cartesian coordinates
(x,y)
.

•

Function dependencies
:

\eta=\eta(x_{1},x_{2})
and
\xi=\xi(x_{1},x_{2})
are scalar functions of horizontal coordinates,
\psi=\psi(x,y)
,
\phi=\phi(x,y)
,
f=f(x,y)
,
g=g(x,y)
are functions defined on the scaled coordinate system.

2.
From water wave to KP-I

2.1.
Dirichlet-Neumann operators

The Dirichlet-Neumann operator (DNO), which maps Dirichlet boundary data to the normal derivative of the solution to Laplace’s equation, plays a fundamental role in water wave theory.

Defining the surface velocity potential as
\xi(\mathbf{x^{\prime}},t)=\Phi(\mathbf{x^{\prime}},\eta(\mathbf{x^{\prime}},t),t)
, the DNO
G(\eta)
is given by

G(\eta)\xi=\Phi_{x_{3}}-\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\eta\cdot\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\Phi.

Using geometric relations on the free surface

\displaystyle\Phi_{x_{3}}-\eta_{x_{1}}\Phi_{x_{1}}-\eta_{x_{2}}\Phi_{x_{2}}

\displaystyle=G(\eta)\xi,

\displaystyle\Phi_{x_{1}}+\eta_{x_{1}}\Phi_{x_{3}}

\displaystyle=\xi_{x_{1}},

\displaystyle\Phi_{x_{2}}+\eta_{x_{2}}\Phi_{x_{3}}

\displaystyle=\xi_{x_{2}},

we solve for the gradient components

\displaystyle\Phi_{x_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(1+\eta_{x_{2}}^{2}\right)\xi_{x_{1}}-\eta_{x_{1}}\eta_{x_{2}}\xi_{x_{2}}-\eta_{x_{1}}G(\eta)\xi}{1+\left|\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\eta\right|^{2}},

\displaystyle\Phi_{x_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(1+\eta_{x_{1}}^{2}\right)\xi_{x_{2}}-\eta_{x_{1}}\eta_{x_{2}}\xi_{x_{1}}-\eta_{x_{2}}G(\eta)\xi}{1+\left|\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\eta\right|^{2}},

\displaystyle\Phi_{x_{3}}

\displaystyle=\frac{G(\eta)\xi+\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\eta\cdot\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\xi}{1+\left|\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\eta\right|^{2}}.

This formulation yields the exact water wave equations

(2.1)

\displaystyle\eta_{t}

\displaystyle=G(\eta)\xi,

\displaystyle\xi_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2(1+|\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\eta|^{2})}\left[\left(G(\eta)\xi+\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\eta\cdot\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\xi\right)^{2}-\left|\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\xi\right|^{2}\left|\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\eta\right|^{2}-\left|\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\xi\right|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle-g\eta+\sigma\operatorname{div}\Big[\frac{\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\eta}{\sqrt{1+|\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\eta|^{2}}}\Big].

2.2.
Asymptotic derivation from water wave equations to KP-I

In this subsection, we establish the asymptotic connection between the water wave system (
2.1
) and the KP-I equation (
1.16
). The derivation proceeds through the following steps:

Introduce a small parameter
\varepsilon
and define rescaled spatial variables

x=\varepsilon x_{1},\quad y=\varepsilon^{2}x_{2}.

Define the perturbation term
\Pi
as

\begin{split}\varepsilon^{6}\Pi:&=\frac{1}{2}\xi_{x_{1}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2(1+|\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\eta|^{2})}\Big[\left(G(\eta)\xi+\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\eta\cdot\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\xi\right)^{2}-\left|\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\xi\right|^{2}\left|\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\eta\right|^{2}-\left|\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\xi\right|^{2}\Big]\\
&\quad+\sigma\Big(\mathrm{div}\Big[\frac{\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\eta}{\sqrt{1+|\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\eta|^{2}}}\Big]-\Delta_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\eta\Big).\end{split}

We consider asymptotic solutions of the form

(2.2)

\displaystyle\eta(x_{1},x_{2},t)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}h\big(\varepsilon(x_{1}-ct),\,\varepsilon^{2}x_{2}\big),

(2.3)

\displaystyle\xi(x_{1},x_{2},t)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon f\big(\varepsilon(x_{1}-ct),\,\varepsilon^{2}x_{2}\big),

with wave speed
c=(1+\varepsilon^{2})^{-1/2}
, gravitational acceleration
g=1
,
and surface tension coefficient
\sigma>\frac{1}{3}
. Here
\varepsilon>0
is a sufficiently small parameter.

Substituting (
2.2
) and (
2.3
) into (
2.1
) yields

(2.4)

\displaystyle-c\partial_{1}h

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-2}G(\varepsilon^{2}h)f,

(2.5)

\displaystyle-c\partial_{1}f

\displaystyle=-h-\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{2}(\partial_{1}f)^{2}+\sigma\varepsilon^{2}(\partial_{1}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{2}^{2})h+\varepsilon^{4}\Pi,

where
\partial_{1}\equiv\partial/\partial x
and
\partial_{2}\equiv\partial/\partial y
denote derivatives with respect to the scaled variables.

Applying
\partial_{1}
to (
2.5
) and combining with (
2.4
) yields

(2.6)

\begin{split}0&=c^{2}\partial_{1}^{2}f+\left(1-\sigma\varepsilon^{2}(\partial_{1}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{2}^{2})\right)\big(\varepsilon^{-2}G(\varepsilon^{2}h)f\big)\\
&\quad-\frac{c}{2}\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{1}(\partial_{1}f)^{2}+c\varepsilon^{4}\partial_{1}\Pi.\end{split}

Define
\mathcal{Q}:=1+\frac{1}{3}\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{P}
where
\mathcal{P}:=-\partial_{1}^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{2}^{2}
.
Applying
\mathcal{Q}
to (
2.6
) gives

\begin{split}0&=c^{2}\left(1+\tfrac{1}{3}\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{P}\right)\partial_{1}^{2}f+(1+\sigma\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{P})\big(\varepsilon^{-2}\mathcal{Q}G(\varepsilon^{2}h)f\big)\\
&\quad-\frac{c}{2}\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{Q}\partial_{1}(\partial_{1}f)^{2}+c\varepsilon^{4}\mathcal{Q}\partial_{1}\Pi.\end{split}

Using the wave speed
c^{2}=\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon^{2}}
, we derive

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\left(1+\tfrac{1}{3}\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{P}\right)\partial_{1}^{2}f+(1+\varepsilon^{2})(1+\sigma\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{P})\left(\varepsilon^{-2}\mathcal{Q}G(\varepsilon^{2}h)f\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2c}\mathcal{Q}\partial_{1}(\partial_{1}f)^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{c}\mathcal{Q}\partial_{1}\Pi

\displaystyle=(1+\varepsilon^{2})(1+\sigma\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{P})\left[\varepsilon^{-2}\mathcal{Q}(G(\varepsilon^{2}h)-G_{0})f\right]+(1+\varepsilon^{2})(1+\sigma\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{P})\left[\varepsilon^{-2}\mathcal{Q}G_{0}f-\mathcal{P}f\right]

\displaystyle\quad+(1+\varepsilon^{2})(1+\sigma\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{P})\mathcal{P}f+\left(1+\tfrac{1}{3}\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{P}\right)\partial_{1}^{2}f

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2c}\mathcal{Q}\partial_{1}(\partial_{1}f)^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{c}\mathcal{Q}\partial_{1}\Pi

\displaystyle=(1+\varepsilon^{2})(1+\sigma\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{P})\left[\varepsilon^{-2}\mathcal{Q}(G(\varepsilon^{2}h)-G_{0})f\right]+(1+\varepsilon^{2})(1+\sigma\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{P})\left[\varepsilon^{-2}\mathcal{Q}G_{0}f-\mathcal{P}f\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{2}\Bigl[-\partial_{1}^{2}f-(1+\varepsilon^{2})\partial_{2}^{2}f+\left(\sigma(1+\varepsilon^{2})-\tfrac{1}{3}\right)\partial_{1}^{4}f+\left(2\sigma(1+\varepsilon^{2})-\tfrac{1}{3}\right)\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{1}^{2}\partial_{2}^{2}f

\displaystyle\quad\qquad+\sigma(1+\varepsilon^{2})\varepsilon^{4}\partial_{2}^{4}f\Bigr]-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2c}\mathcal{Q}\partial_{1}(\partial_{1}f)^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{c}\mathcal{Q}\partial_{1}\Pi.

Define the operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1}

\displaystyle:=A\partial_{1}^{4}-\partial_{1}^{2}-(1+\varepsilon^{2})\partial_{2}^{2}+\left(2A+\tfrac{1}{3}\right)\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{1}^{2}\partial_{2}^{2}+\sigma(1+\varepsilon^{2})\varepsilon^{4}\partial_{2}^{4},

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{2}

\displaystyle:=\varepsilon^{-4}(1+\varepsilon^{2})(1+\varepsilon^{2}\sigma\mathcal{P})(\mathcal{Q}G_{0}-\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{P}),

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle:=\sigma(1+\varepsilon^{2})-\tfrac{1}{3}>0.

Applying the expansion
G(\varepsilon^{2}h)f=G_{0}f+G_{1}(\varepsilon^{2}h)f+R_{2}(\varepsilon^{2}h)f
yields

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{1}f+\mathcal{L}_{2}f-\frac{1}{2c}\mathcal{Q}\partial_{1}(\partial_{1}f)^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{c}\mathcal{Q}\partial_{1}\Pi

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{-2}(1+\varepsilon^{2})(1+\sigma\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{P})\left[\varepsilon^{-2}\mathcal{Q}(G(\varepsilon^{2}h)-G_{0})f\right]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{1}f+\mathcal{L}_{2}f-\frac{1}{2c}\mathcal{Q}\partial_{1}(\partial_{1}f)^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{c}\mathcal{Q}\partial_{1}\Pi

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{-4}(1+\varepsilon^{2})(1+\sigma\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{P})\mathcal{Q}R_{2}(\varepsilon^{2}h)f+\varepsilon^{-2}(1+\varepsilon^{2})(1+\sigma\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{P})\mathcal{Q}G_{1}(h)f.

From (
2.5
), we calculate

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-2}(1+\varepsilon^{2})(1+\sigma\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{P})\mathcal{Q}G_{1}(h)f

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-2}(1+\varepsilon^{2})\Bigl(-G_{0}hG_{0}f-\varepsilon^{4}\partial_{2}(h\partial_{2}f)-\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{1}(h\partial_{1}f)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\quad+(1+\varepsilon^{2})\Bigl[\left(\tfrac{1}{3}+\sigma\right)\mathcal{P}G_{1}(h)f+\tfrac{\sigma}{3}\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{P}^{2}G_{1}(h)f\Bigr]

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon^{-2}(1+\varepsilon^{2})\Bigl(G_{0}hG_{0}f+\varepsilon^{4}\partial_{2}(h\partial_{2}f)\Bigr)+(1+\varepsilon^{2})\Bigl[\left(\tfrac{1}{3}+\sigma\right)\mathcal{P}G_{1}(h)f+\tfrac{\sigma}{3}\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{P}^{2}G_{1}(h)f\Bigr]

\displaystyle\quad-(1+\varepsilon^{2})\Bigl(c\partial_{1}(\partial_{1}f)^{2}-\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\partial_{1}((\partial_{1}f)^{3})+\varepsilon^{4}\partial_{1}((\partial_{1}f)\Pi)-\sigma\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{1}((\partial_{1}f)\mathcal{P}h)\Bigr).

This leads to

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1}f+\mathcal{L}_{2}f-\frac{1}{2c}\mathcal{Q}\partial_{1}(\partial_{1}f)^{2}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon^{-4}(1+\varepsilon^{2})(1+\sigma\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{P})\mathcal{Q}R_{2}(\varepsilon^{2}h)f+\varepsilon^{-2}(1+\varepsilon^{2})\Bigl(G_{0}hG_{0}f+\varepsilon^{4}\partial_{2}(h\partial_{2}f)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\quad-(1+\varepsilon^{2})\Bigl[\left(\tfrac{1}{3}+\sigma\right)\mathcal{P}G_{1}(h)f+\tfrac{\sigma}{3}\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{P}^{2}G_{1}(h)f\Bigr]-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{c}\mathcal{Q}\partial_{1}\Pi

\displaystyle\quad+(1+\varepsilon^{2})\Bigl(c\partial_{1}(\partial_{1}f)^{2}-\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\partial_{1}((\partial_{1}f)^{3})+\varepsilon^{4}\partial_{1}((\partial_{1}f)\Pi)-\sigma\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{1}((\partial_{1}f)\mathcal{P}h)\Bigr),

where the nonlinear term expands as

(2.7)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2c}\mathcal{Q}\partial_{1}(\partial_{1}f)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2c}\partial_{1}(\partial_{1}f)^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{6c}\mathcal{P}\partial_{1}(\partial_{1}f)^{2},

the remainder terms satisfy

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-4}(1+\sigma\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{P})(\mathcal{Q}{R}_{2}(\varepsilon^{2}h)f)+\tfrac{1}{3}\sigma\varepsilon^{2}\mathcal{P}^{2}G_{1}(h)f

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-4}G_{2}(\varepsilon^{2}h)f+\varepsilon^{-4}R_{3}(\varepsilon^{2}h)f+\varepsilon^{-2}\left(\sigma+\tfrac{1}{3}\right)\mathcal{P}G_{2}(\varepsilon^{2}h)f

(2.8)

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{-2}\left(\sigma+\tfrac{1}{3}\right)\mathcal{P}{R}_{3}(\varepsilon^{2}h)f+\tfrac{1}{3}\sigma\mathcal{P}^{2}{R}_{1}(\varepsilon^{2}h)f,

and the principal operator acts as

(2.9)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{P}G_{1}(h)f

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}\Bigl(G_{0}(hG_{0}f)+\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{1}(h\partial_{1}f)+\varepsilon^{4}\partial_{2}(h\partial_{2}f)\Bigr).

Finally, we obtain the asymptotic equation

(2.10)

\mathcal{L}_{1}f+\mathcal{L}_{2}f-\frac{3}{2c}\partial_{1}(\partial_{1}f)^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{4}P_{i},

where the perturbation terms are defined as follows

\displaystyle P_{1}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{c}\partial_{1}(\mathcal{Q}\Pi)-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2c^{2}}\partial_{1}((\partial_{1}f)^{3})-\frac{\sigma\varepsilon^{2}}{c^{2}}\partial_{1}\bigl((\partial_{1}f)(\mathcal{P}h)\bigr)+\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{c^{2}}\partial_{1}\bigl((\partial_{1}f)\Pi\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{6c}\mathcal{P}\partial_{1}(\partial_{1}f)^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{c^{2}}\left(\tfrac{1}{3}+\sigma\right)\mathcal{P}\partial_{1}(h\partial_{1}f),

\displaystyle P_{2}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{c^{2}}\partial_{2}(h\partial_{2}f)+\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{c^{2}}\left(\tfrac{1}{3}+\sigma\right)\mathcal{P}\partial_{2}(h\partial_{2}f),

\displaystyle P_{3}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{\varepsilon^{-4}}{c^{2}}G_{2}(\varepsilon^{2}h)f-\frac{\varepsilon^{-4}}{c^{2}}R_{3}(\varepsilon^{2}h)f-\frac{\varepsilon^{-2}}{c^{2}}\left(\sigma+\tfrac{1}{3}\right)\mathcal{P}G_{2}(\varepsilon^{2}h)f

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\varepsilon^{-2}}{c^{2}}\left(\sigma+\tfrac{1}{3}\right)\mathcal{P}{R}_{3}(\varepsilon^{2}h)f-\frac{1}{3c^{2}}\sigma\mathcal{P}^{2}{R}_{1}(\varepsilon^{2}h)f,

\displaystyle P_{4}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\varepsilon^{-2}}{c^{2}}(G_{0}hG_{0}f)+\frac{1}{c^{2}}\left(\tfrac{1}{3}+\sigma\right)\mathcal{P}G_{0}hG_{0}f.

Remark 2.1
.

The systematic analysis of Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator (DNO) estimation will be presented in Section 3. Specifically:

•

Subsections 3.1 and 3.2 establish spectral decompositions of the principal operators
G_{0}
,
G_{1}
, and
G_{2}
using Fourier analysis techniques

•

Subsection 3.3 characterizes the Sobolev regularity properties of the residual operator
R_{3}

Regarding the left-hand side of (
2.10
), we seek a solution of the form
f=q+\phi
, where
q=q_{\varepsilon}
is a lump solution of the KP-I equation

\displaystyle A\partial_{x}^{4}q-\partial_{x}^{2}q-(1+\varepsilon^{2})\partial_{y}^{2}q-\frac{3}{2c}\partial_{x}((\partial_{x}q)^{2})=0.

Throughout the proof, we use the standard lump solution

(2.11)

\displaystyle q_{\varepsilon}(x,y)=-\frac{8\sqrt{1+\varepsilon^{2}}Ax}{y^{2}+(1+\varepsilon^{2})(x^{2}+3A)},

whose nondegeneracy is established in
[
46
]
.

The unique negative eigenvalue and corresponding eigenfunction for
q_{\varepsilon}
are closely approximated by those of the limiting equation (as
\varepsilon\to 0
):

(2.12)

\displaystyle(\sigma-\tfrac{1}{3})\partial_{x}^{4}q-\partial_{x}^{2}q-\partial_{y}^{2}q-\frac{3}{2}\partial_{x}((\partial_{x}q)^{2})=0,

with solution
q_{0}(x,y)=-\dfrac{8\left(\sigma-\frac{1}{3}\right)x}{x^{2}+y^{2}+3\sigma-1}
. Consequently, the linearized problem for (
2.10
) is well-approximated by that of the KP-I equation.

3.
Methodological adaptation for DNO operator estimation

In this section, we will estimate the Dirichlet-to-Neumann (DNO) operator. The primary methodology draws inspiration from Craig’s foundational framework
[
21
]
, with targeted modifications to enhance compatibility with subsequent nonlinear term analysis. Specifically, we refine the original operator estimation scheme by introducing a norm adaptation strategy, enabling higher flexibility in bounding conditions while addressing the inherent complexity trade-offs. This adaptation ensures that the operators
G_{0},G_{1},G_{2}
and
R_{3}

satisfy the regularity requirements for the nonlinear regime in Section
5
.

To facilitate the subsequent proofs in this section, we first introduce the description of the Dirichlet-Neumann operator (DNO) from
[
21
,
62
]
. Let
{\bf x}^{\prime},{\bf y}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and consider functions
\eta:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\xi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
.
An exact implicit formula for the Dirichlet-Neumann operator is given by

\displaystyle(1-B(\eta))G(\eta)\xi=|D|\tanh(|D|)\xi+A(\eta)\xi,

where

\displaystyle A(\eta)\xi

\displaystyle=-|D|(1+e^{-2|D|})^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}m({\bf x}^{\prime},{\bf y}^{\prime})\xi({\bf y}^{\prime})d{\bf y}^{\prime}\equiv(A_{1}+\ldots+A_{L}+A_{L+1}^{R})\xi,

\displaystyle B(\eta)\xi

\displaystyle=-|D|(1+e^{-2|D|})^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}l({\bf x}^{\prime},{\bf y}^{\prime})\xi({\bf y}^{\prime})d{\bf y}^{\prime}\equiv(B_{1}+\ldots+B_{L}+B_{L+1}^{R})\xi,

with

\displaystyle m({\bf x}^{\prime},{\bf y}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{-({\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime})\cdot\partial_{{\bf y}^{\prime}}P}{|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}|^{2}}\frac{1}{(1+P^{2})^{3/2}}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\Big[\frac{({\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime})\cdot\partial_{{\bf y}^{\prime}}Q}{|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4}+\frac{4Q}{(|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{2}}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad\times\frac{1}{(1+\frac{4}{(|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{1/2}}Q+Q^{2})^{3/2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{2}{(|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{3/2}}\Big[\frac{1}{(1+\frac{4}{(\left|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}\right|^{2}+4)^{1/2}}Q+Q^{2})^{3/2}}-1\Big],

\displaystyle l({\bf x}^{\prime},{\bf y}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{1}{|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}|}\Big(\frac{1}{(1+P^{2})^{1/2}}-1\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{1}{(\left|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}\right|^{2}+4)^{1/2}}\Big[\frac{1}{(1+\frac{4}{(|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{1/2}}Q+Q^{2})^{1/2}}-1\Big],

where

\displaystyle P(\eta)=\frac{\eta\left({\bf x}^{\prime}\right)-\eta\left({\bf y}^{\prime}\right)}{\left|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}\right|},\quad Q(\eta)=\frac{\eta\left({\bf x}^{\prime}\right)+\eta\left({\bf y}^{\prime}\right)}{(\left|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}\right|^{2}+4)^{1/2}},\quad\kappa_{h}\left({\bf x}^{\prime},{\bf y}^{\prime}\right)=\frac{4}{(\left|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}\right|^{2}+4)^{1/2}},

and

\displaystyle A_{l}\xi

\displaystyle=-|D|(1+e^{-2|D|})^{-1}\frac{1}{2\pi}

\displaystyle\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Big[\frac{({\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime})\cdot\partial_{{\bf y}^{\prime}}\tilde{p}_{l}(P)}{|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}|^{2}}+\Big(\frac{({\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime})\cdot\partial_{{\bf y}^{\prime}}Q}{|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4}+\frac{4Q}{(|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{2}}\Big)\tilde{q}_{l-1}(Q,\kappa_{h})

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{2}{(|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{3/2}}\tilde{q}_{l}(Q,\kappa_{h})\Big]\xi({\bf y}^{\prime})d{\bf y}^{\prime},

\displaystyle A_{L+1}^{R}\xi

\displaystyle=-|D|(1+e^{-2|D|})^{-1}\frac{1}{2\pi}

\displaystyle\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Big[\frac{({\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime})\cdot\partial_{{\bf y}^{\prime}}\tilde{p}^{R}_{L+1}(P)}{|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}|^{2}}+\Big(\frac{({\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime})\cdot\partial_{{\bf y}^{\prime}}Q}{|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4}+\frac{4Q}{(|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{2}}\Big)\tilde{q}^{R}_{L}(Q,\kappa_{h})

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{2}{(|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{3/2}}\tilde{q}^{R}_{L+1}(Q,\kappa_{h})\Big]\xi({\bf y}^{\prime})d{\bf y}^{\prime},

\displaystyle B_{l}\xi

\displaystyle=-|D|(1+e^{-2|D|})^{-1}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Big(\frac{p_{l}(P)}{\left|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}\right|}+\frac{q_{l}\left(Q,\kappa_{h}\right)}{(|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{1/2}}\Big)\xi d{\bf y}^{\prime},

\displaystyle B_{L+1}^{R}\xi

\displaystyle=-|D|(1+e^{-2|D|})^{-1}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Big(\frac{p_{L+1}^{R}(P)}{|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}|}+\frac{q_{L+1}^{R}(Q,\kappa_{h})}{(|{\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{1/2}}\Big)\xi d{\bf y}^{\prime}.

Here,
{p}_{l}(\text{for }l\geq 2\text{ for }l\text{ even})
and
q_{l}(\text{for }l\geq 1)
are homogeneous polynomials of order
l
in
\eta
, derived from the expansion of

\frac{1}{(1+\sigma)^{1/2}}-1=-\frac{1}{2}\sigma+\frac{3}{8}\sigma^{2}-\ldots

where
\sigma
corresponds to
P^{2}
and
\kappa_{h}Q+Q^{2}
, respectively. Similarly,
\tilde{p}_{l}(\text{for }l\geq 1\text{ for }l\text{ odd})
and
\tilde{q}_{l}(\text{for }l\geq 1)
are homogeneous polynomials of order
l
in
\eta
, with
\tilde{p}_{l}

additionally involving derivatives of
\eta
.
The remainders of these expansions, denoted by

p_{L+1}^{R},q_{L+1}^{R},\tilde{p}_{L+1}^{R}
and
\tilde{q}_{L+1}^{R}
, are expressions of order
\mathcal{O}\left(\eta^{L+1}\right)
for small
\eta
.

Remark 3.1
.

We observe that the operators
(1+e^{-2|D|})^{-1}
and the Riesz potential
R_{j}(D)=iD_{j}/|D|
in the previously defined
A_{l}
and
B_{l}
are bounded on the Sobolev space
W^{s,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
1<q<\infty
and non-negative integers
s\geq 0
. This boundedness follows from the Mikhlin multiplier theorem (see Appendix A for details), as both symbols satisfy the Mikhlin condition:

\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}}\big|\xi^{|\alpha|}\partial^{\alpha}\sigma(\xi)\big|\leq C_{\alpha},\quad\forall|\alpha|\leq 2,

where
\sigma
denotes the symbol of either operator. This property is particularly advantageous for subsequent a priori estimates in our analysis, as it ensures uniform control of higher-order derivatives.

Since the most fundamental part of the DNO operator estimation involves singular integral operators, we first define them as follows. Let two operators be defined as:

\begin{split}S_{l}(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{l})\xi({\bf x}^{\prime})&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K({\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime})\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{l}P(\eta_{j})\Big)\xi({\bf y}^{\prime})d{\bf y}^{\prime},\\
S_{l,*}(\eta,\ldots,\eta)&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K_{*}\left({\bf x}^{\prime}-{\bf y}^{\prime}\right)\kappa_{h}^{r}Q^{l}(\eta)\xi({\bf y}^{\prime})d{\bf y}^{\prime}\end{split}

where
K
is a Calderón–Zygmund kernel satisfying standard estimates, and
K_{*}
takes the form:

K_{*}({\bf x}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{(|{\bf x}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{\rho/2}}\prod_{l=1}^{2}\Big(\frac{x_{l}}{(|{\bf x}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{1/2}}\Big)^{m_{l}},\text{ for }l+\rho+r>2.

To establish the desired estimates, we require the following lemmas, which can be found in
[
21
,
62
]
. In what follows, the constants
C
, which appear below, are related only to
s
, and are independent of
\eta
and
\xi
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Suppose that
\eta\in C^{s+1}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\right)
, and consider the mapping properties of the operator
S_{l}(\eta,\cdots,\eta)
. Let
1<q<\infty
, then we have the estimate that

\left\|\partial_{x}^{s}S_{l}(\eta,\cdots,\eta)\xi\right\|_{q}\leq Cl^{M+s}\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}^{l-1}\big(\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}\left\|\partial_{x}^{s}\xi\right\|_{q}+\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}\|\xi\|_{q}\big).

Lemma 3.2
.

Suppose
l+\rho+r>2
, then we have the estimates

\|\partial_{x}^{s}S_{l,*}(\eta,\ldots,\eta)\xi\|_{q}\leq Cl^{s}\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}}^{l-1}\|\eta\|_{C^{s}}\|\xi\|_{q}.

3.1.
Estimates for the operator
B_{l}

We now establish refined estimates for the operators
B_{l}
, which are crucial for our subsequent analysis.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
1<q<\infty
with
\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{q_{1}}+\frac{1}{q_{2}}
,
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and
\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}<\frac{1}{4}
. For integers
l\geq 1
and
L\geq 1
, the operators
B_{l}
and
B_{L}^{R}
satisfy:

\displaystyle\big\||D|^{-1}B_{1}(\eta)\xi\big\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\xi\|_{q_{2}},

\displaystyle\big\||D|^{-1}B_{l}(\eta)\xi\big\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C\,2^{l}l^{M+s}\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}^{l-2}\Big(\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}\|\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}\|\xi\|_{q_{2}}\Big),\quad l\geq 2,

and for the remainder term,

\displaystyle\big\||D|^{-1}B_{L}^{R}(\eta)\xi\big\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C\,2^{L}L^{M+s}\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}^{L-2}\Big(\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}\|\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}\|\xi\|_{q_{2}}\Big),\quad L\geq 2.

Furthermore, the full operator satisfies

\big\||D|^{-1}B(\eta)\xi\big\|_{s,q}\leq C\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\Big(\|\xi\|_{q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}\|\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}\|\xi\|_{q_{2}}\Big).

Proof.

Recall the definitions of the operators:

\displaystyle|D|^{-1}B(\eta)\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-(1+e^{-2|D|})^{-1}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|}\left(\frac{1}{(1+P^{2})^{1/2}}-1\right)\xi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})d\mathbf{y}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\quad-(1+e^{-2|D|})^{-1}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{(|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{1/2}}\left(\frac{1}{(1+\kappa_{h}Q+Q^{2})^{1/2}}-1\right)\xi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})d\mathbf{y}^{\prime},

\displaystyle|D|^{-1}B_{1}(\eta)\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(1+e^{-2|D|})^{-1}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\eta(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})+\eta(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})}{(|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{3/2}}\xi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})d\mathbf{y}^{\prime},

\displaystyle|D|^{-1}B_{l}(\eta)\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-\big(1+e^{-2|D|}\big)^{-1}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\frac{p_{l}(P)}{|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|}+\frac{q_{l}(Q,\kappa_{h})}{(|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{1/2}}\right)\xi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})d\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\quad\text{for}\quad l\geq 2,

where
P=P(\eta)
and
Q=Q(\eta)
are auxiliary functions depending on
\eta
. The series expansions are given by

\frac{1}{(1+P^{2})^{1/2}}-1=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\geq 2\\
l\text{ even}\end{subarray}}^{\infty}p_{l}(P),\quad p_{l}(P)=a_{l}P^{l},\quad a_{l}=\Big(\frac{-1}{4}\Big)^{l/2}\binom{l}{l/2},

and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1+\kappa_{h}Q+Q^{2})^{1/2}}-1=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}q_{l}(Q,\kappa_{h}),

\displaystyle q_{l}(Q,\kappa_{h})=\sum_{j=0}^{[l/2]}b_{l-j}\kappa_{h}^{l-2j}Q^{l},\quad b_{l-j}=\Big(\frac{-1}{4}\Big)^{l-j}\binom{2l-2j}{l-j}\binom{l-j}{j}.

By Remark
3.1
, it suffices to analyze the following two types of integral operators in
B_{l}
.

Case 1: Operator
B_{1}(\eta)
.

Consider the convolution kernel
M(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=(|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{-3/2}
. We estimate

\displaystyle\big\||D|^{-1}B_{1}(\eta)\xi\big\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C\big\|\eta(M*\xi)+M*(\eta\xi)\big\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|M*\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|M*(\eta\xi)\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\xi\|_{q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{q_{1}}\|\xi\|_{q_{2}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\xi\|_{q_{2}},

where the last inequality follows from the embedding
\|\eta\|_{q_{1}}\leq C\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}
since
s\geq 0
.

Case 2: Operators
B_{l}(\eta)
for
l\geq 2
.

We decompose the operator into two components:

\displaystyle P_{l}(\eta)\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|}p_{l}(P)\xi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})d\mathbf{y}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=a_{l}\eta(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|^{2}}P^{l-1}\xi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})d\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-a_{l}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|^{2}}P^{l-1}\eta(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\xi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})d\mathbf{y}^{\prime}.

This corresponds to the sum
a_{l}\eta S_{l-1}\xi+a_{l}S_{l-1}(\eta\xi)
with kernel
|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|^{-2}
. Applying Lemma
3.1
and Stirling’s approximation
\binom{2n}{n}\sim 2^{2n}/\sqrt{\pi n}
yields

\displaystyle\|P_{l}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq\|\eta S_{l-1}\xi\|_{s,q}+\|S_{l-1}(\eta\xi)\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|S_{l-1}\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+Cl^{M+s}\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}^{l-2}\Big(\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}\|\eta\xi\|_{s,q}+\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}\|\eta\xi\|_{q}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq Cl^{M+s}\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}^{l-2}\Big(\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}\|\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}\|\xi\|_{q_{2}}\Big).

Similarly, for the
Q
-component:

\displaystyle Q_{l,*}(\eta)\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{(|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{1/2}}q_{l}(Q,\kappa_{h})\xi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})d\mathbf{y}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor l/2\rfloor}b_{l-j}\eta(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4}\kappa_{h}^{l-2j}Q^{l-1}\xi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})d\mathbf{y}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor l/2\rfloor}b_{l-j}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4}\kappa_{h}^{l-2j}Q^{l-1}\eta(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\xi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})d\mathbf{y}^{\prime}.

This is a sum of operators
\eta S_{l-1,*}\xi
and
S_{l-1,*}(\eta\xi)
. Applying Lemma
3.2
for
l\geq 2
gives

\displaystyle\|Q_{l,*}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{l}\Big(\|\eta S_{l-1,*}\xi\|_{s,q}+\|S_{l-1,*}(\eta\xi)\|_{s,q}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C2^{l}l^{s}\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}}^{l-2}\|\eta\|_{C^{s}}\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\xi\|_{q_{2}}.

Combining both components:

\displaystyle\big\||D|^{-1}B_{l}(\eta)\xi\big\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq\|P_{l}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}+\|Q_{l,*}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C2^{l}l^{M+s}\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}^{l-2}\Big(\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}\|\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}\|\xi\|_{q_{2}}\Big).

Remainder estimate for
B_{L}^{R}(\eta)
.

For the remainder term, we have

\displaystyle\big\||D|^{-1}B_{L}^{R}(\eta)\xi\big\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=L+1}^{\infty}\big\||D|^{-1}B_{j}(\eta)\xi\big\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C2^{L}L^{M+s}\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}^{L-2}\Big(\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}\|\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}\|\xi\|_{q_{2}}\Big),

where the summation converges under the condition
\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}<1/4
.

This completes the proof of Theorem
3.1
.
∎

In the proof of the above theorem, we establish the following corollary.

Corollary 3.1
.

Let
1<q<\infty
,
\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{q_{1}}+\frac{1}{q_{2}}
,
s\in\mathbb{Z}
with
s\geq 0
, and
\|\eta_{i}\|_{C^{1}}<\frac{1}{4}
for
i=1,2
. For
l\geq 1
,
L\geq 1
, and
\eta_{t}=\eta_{1}+t(\eta_{2}-\eta_{1})
with
t\in[0,1]
, the operators
B_{l}
and
B_{L}^{R}
satisfy the estimates

\displaystyle\left\||D|^{-1}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}B_{1}(\eta_{t})\xi\right\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\eta_{2}-\eta_{1}\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\xi\|_{q_{2}},

\displaystyle\left\||D|^{-1}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}B_{l}(\eta_{t})\xi\right\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C2^{l}l^{M+s+1}\|\eta_{2}-\eta_{1}\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\eta_{t}\|_{C^{1}}^{l-2}

\displaystyle\quad\times\big(\|\eta_{t}\|_{C^{1}}\|\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta_{t}\|_{C^{s+1}}\|\xi\|_{q_{2}}\big),\quad l\geq 2,

and

\displaystyle\left\||D|^{-1}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}B_{L}^{R}(\eta_{t})\xi\right\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C2^{L}L^{M+s+1}\|\eta_{2}-\eta_{1}\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\eta_{t}\|_{C^{1}}^{L-2}

\displaystyle\quad\times\big(\|\eta_{t}\|_{C^{1}}\|\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta_{t}\|_{C^{s+1}}\|\xi\|_{q_{2}}\big),\quad L\geq 2.

Moreover,

\left\||D|^{-1}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}B(\eta_{t})\xi\right\|_{s,q}\leq C\|\eta_{2}-\eta_{1}\|_{s,q_{1}}\Big(\|\xi\|_{q_{2}}+\|\eta_{t}\|_{C^{1}}\|\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta_{t}\|_{C^{s+1}}\|\xi\|_{q_{2}}\Big).

Proof.

We first establish the estimate for
B_{1}
. Direct calculation shows that

\displaystyle|D|^{-1}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}B_{1}(\eta_{t})\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=(1+e^{-2|D|})^{-1}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{(\eta_{2}-\eta_{1})(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})+(\eta_{2}-\eta_{1})(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})}{(|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{3/2}}\xi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}.

The kernel
\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})=(|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{-3/2}
defines a bounded integral operator on the relevant function spaces. Combining this with the boundedness of the Fourier multiplier
(1+e^{-2|D|})^{-1}
on Sobolev spaces, we obtain

\left\||D|^{-1}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}B_{1}(\eta_{t})\xi\right\|_{s,q}\leq C\|\eta_{2}-\eta_{1}\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\xi\|_{q_{2}}.

For
l\geq 2
and
L\geq 2
, the estimates for
B_{l}
and
B_{L}^{R}
follow by applying the same techniques used in the proof of Theorem
3.1
, where we differentiate the operator series and control the resulting expressions using the norm bounds for
\eta_{t}
and
\xi
.
∎

For the operator
B_{l}
, we establish the following estimates.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
1<q<\infty
,
\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{q_{1}}+\frac{1}{q_{2}}
,
s\in\mathbb{Z}
with
s\geq 0
, and
\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}<\frac{1}{4}
. For
l\geq 1
and
L\geq 1
, the following estimates hold:

\displaystyle\|B_{1}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\|\partial_{x}\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\partial_{x}\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}\right),

\displaystyle\|B_{l}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C2^{l}l^{M+s+1}\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}^{l-2}\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}\left(\|\partial_{x}\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\partial_{x}\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}\right),\quad l\geq 2,

\displaystyle\|B_{L}^{R}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C2^{L}L^{M+s+1}\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}^{L-2}\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}\left(\|\partial_{x}\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\partial_{x}\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}\right),\quad L\geq 2.

Moreover, for the full operator
B(\eta)=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}B_{l}(\eta)
, we have

\|B(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}\leq C\left(1+\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}\right)\left(\|\partial_{x}\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\partial_{x}\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}\right).

Proof.

Recall the operator expressions:

\displaystyle B_{1}(\eta)\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=|D|(1+e^{-2|D|})^{-1}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\eta(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})+\eta(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})}{(|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{3/2}}\xi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}^{\prime},

\displaystyle B_{l}(\eta)\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-|D|(1+e^{-2|D|})^{-1}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\frac{p_{l}(P)}{|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|}+\frac{q_{l}(Q,\kappa_{h})}{(|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{1/2}}\right)\xi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}^{\prime},

where
p_{l}(P)=a_{l}P^{l}
and
q_{l}(Q,\kappa_{h})=\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor l/2\rfloor}b_{l-j}\kappa_{h}^{l-2j}Q^{l}
.

Part 1: Estimate for
B_{1}(\eta)

Denote the kernel
M(\mathbf{x^{\prime}})=(|\mathbf{x^{\prime}}|^{2}+4)^{-3/2}
.
We have

\displaystyle\|B_{1}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C\big\|\partial_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\big[\eta(M*\xi)\big]\big\|_{s,q}+C\big\|\partial_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\big[M*(\eta\xi)\big]\big\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\partial_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|M*\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+C\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\partial_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(M*\xi)\|_{s,q_{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+C\|M*\partial_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(\eta\xi)\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\partial_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+C\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\partial_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}.

Part 2: Estimate for
B_{l}(\eta)
with
l\geq 2

We decompose
B_{l}(\eta)\xi=-|D|(1+e^{-2|D|})^{-1}\frac{1}{2\pi}\big[P_{l}(\eta)\xi+Q_{l,*}(\eta)\xi\big]
where:

\displaystyle P_{l}(\eta)\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|^{-1}p_{l}(P)\xi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}^{\prime},

\displaystyle Q_{l,*}(\eta)\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{-1/2}q_{l}(Q,\kappa_{h})\xi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}.

Step 2.1: Derivative of
P_{l}(\eta)\xi

Differentiating
P_{l}(\eta)\xi
yields

\displaystyle\partial_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}P_{l}(\eta)\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=la_{l}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|^{-2}P^{l-1}\big(\partial_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\eta(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})-\partial_{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}}\eta(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\big)\xi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\quad+a_{l}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|^{-2}P^{l-1}\big(\eta(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})-\eta(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\big)\partial_{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}}\xi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}.

This decomposes into four operators:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{1}

\displaystyle=(\partial_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\eta)S_{l-1}\xi,\quad\mathcal{T}_{2}=S_{l-1}((\partial_{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}}\eta)\xi),

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{3}

\displaystyle=\eta S_{l-1}(\partial_{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}}\xi),\quad\mathcal{T}_{4}=S_{l-1}(\eta\partial_{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}}\xi).

Applying Lemma
3.1
and using
\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}<1/4
gives

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}P_{l}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq Cl\big(\|\mathcal{T}_{1}\|_{s,q}+\|\mathcal{T}_{2}\|_{s,q}+\|\mathcal{T}_{3}\|_{s,q}+\|\mathcal{T}_{4}\|_{s,q}\big)

\displaystyle\leq Cl^{M+s+1}\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}^{l-2}\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}\Big(\|\partial_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\partial_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}\Big).

Step 2.2: Derivative of
Q_{l,*}(\eta)\xi

Differentiating
Q_{l,*}(\eta)\xi
yields

\displaystyle\partial_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}Q_{l,*}(\eta)\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=l\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor l/2\rfloor}b_{l-j}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{-1}\kappa_{h}^{l-2j}Q^{l-1}\big(\partial_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\eta(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})+\partial_{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}}\eta(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\big)\xi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor l/2\rfloor}b_{l-j}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|^{2}+4)^{-1}\kappa_{h}^{l-2j}Q^{l-1}\big(\eta(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})+\eta(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\big)\partial_{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}}\xi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}.

This decomposes into:

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{1}

\displaystyle=(\partial_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\eta)S_{l-1,*}\xi,\quad\mathcal{U}_{2}=S_{l-1,*}(\partial_{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}}\eta\xi),

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{3}

\displaystyle=\eta S_{l-1,*}(\partial_{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}}\xi),\quad\mathcal{U}_{4}=S_{l-1,*}(\eta\partial_{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}}\xi).

Applying Lemma
3.2
and using
\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}<1/4
gives

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}Q_{l,*}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C2^{l}l\big(\|\mathcal{U}_{1}\|_{s,q}+\|\mathcal{U}_{2}\|_{s,q}+\|\mathcal{U}_{3}\|_{s,q}+\|\mathcal{U}_{4}\|_{s,q}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C2^{l}l^{s+1}\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}^{l-2}\|\eta\|_{C^{s}}\Big(\|\partial_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\xi\|_{q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\partial_{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}}\xi\|_{q_{2}}\Big).

Step 2.3: Combining estimates

Since
(1+e^{-2|D|})^{-1}
is bounded on
W^{s,q}
, we combine both components:

\displaystyle\|B_{l}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\|\partial_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}P_{l}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}+\|\partial_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}Q_{l,*}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C2^{l}l^{M+s+1}\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}^{l-2}\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}\Big(\|\partial_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\partial_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}\Big).

Part 3: Estimate for
B_{L}^{R}(\eta)

The bound for
B_{L}^{R}(\eta)
follows identically to
B_{l}(\eta)
using the methodology from Theorem
3.1
, as the residual terms satisfy analogous kernel estimates.

Part 4: Estimate for
B(\eta)

Summing over
l\geq 1
and using
\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}<1/4
for convergence:

\displaystyle\|B(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\|B_{l}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C\big(1+\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}\big)\Big(\|\partial_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\partial_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}\Big),

where the series converges absolutely since
\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}<1/4
implies geometric decay.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

In
[
21
,
62
]
, the estimates for the operators
B_{l}
and
B_{L}^{R}
are given as follows:
Let
1<q<\infty
and
0\leq s\in\mathbb{Z}
. The operators
B_{l}
and
B_{L}^{R}
satisfy

\left\|B_{l}\xi\right\|_{s,q}\leq C\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}^{l-1}\left(\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}\|\xi\|_{q}+\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}\|\xi\|_{s,q}\right)

and

\|B_{L}^{R}\xi\|_{s,q}\leq C\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}^{L-1}\left(\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}\|\xi\|_{q}+\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}\|\xi\|_{s,q}\right).

The lemma established above enables us to refine these estimates for practical applications.

In estimating the remainder term
R_{3}
, we require the following estimates for
B^{m}
, which follow directly from Remark
3.2
:

Lemma 3.3
.

The powers of
B
satisfy

\|B^{m}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}\leq C\left(1+C_{0}\right)^{m-1}\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}^{m-1}\big(\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}\|\xi\|_{q}+\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}\|\xi\|_{s,q}\big).

3.2.
Estimates for the operator
A_{l}

We now estimate the operator
A_{l}
, which is derived from the representation introduced in Section
3
and plays a pivotal role in bounding the residual term
R_{3}
.

First, we state a lemma essential for estimating
A_{l}
; detailed proofs can be found in
[
21
,
62
]
.

Lemma 3.4
.

For
{\bf x^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
f(P)
is an old continuous function of
P
and
\eta\in

C^{1}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\right)
, we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}}{\left|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}\right|^{2}}\cdot\partial_{{\bf y^{\prime}}}(f(P))\xi\left({\bf y^{\prime}}\right)d{\bf y^{\prime}}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}}{\left|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}\right|^{2}}f(P)\cdot\partial_{{\bf y^{\prime}}}\xi\left({\bf y^{\prime}}\right)d{\bf y^{\prime}}.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{q_{1}}+\frac{1}{q_{2}}
and
\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}}<1/4
. The operators
A_{l}
and
A_{L}^{R}
satisfy the following estimates for all integers
l\geq 2
and
L\geq 2
:

\left\||D|^{-1}A_{l}(\eta)\xi\right\|_{s,q}\leq C2^{l}l^{M+s}\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}^{l-2}\|\eta\|_{\max\{s,1\},q_{1}}\left(\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}\left\|\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\xi\right\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}\|\xi\|_{1,q_{2}}\right),

and

\left\||D|^{-1}A_{L}^{R}(\eta)\xi\right\|_{s,q}\leq C2^{L}L^{M+s}\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}^{L-2}\|\eta\|_{\max\{s,1\},q_{1}}\left(\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}\left\|\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\xi\right\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}\|\xi\|_{1,q_{2}}\right).

Proof.

We recall the integral representation of
|D|^{-1}A(\eta)\xi
:

\displaystyle|D|^{-1}A(\eta)\xi

\displaystyle=(1+e^{-2|D|})^{-1}\frac{1}{2\pi}\Bigg[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{({\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}})\cdot\partial_{{\bf y^{\prime}}}P}{|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}}\frac{\xi({\bf y^{\prime}})}{(1+P^{2})^{3/2}}d{\bf y^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\frac{({\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}})\cdot\partial_{{\bf y^{\prime}}}Q}{|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}+4}+\frac{4Q}{(|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}+4)^{2}}\right)\frac{\xi({\bf y^{\prime}})}{(1+\kappa_{h}Q+Q^{2})^{3/2}}d{\bf y^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{2}{(|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}+4)^{3/2}}\left[\frac{1}{(1+\kappa_{h}Q+Q^{2})^{3/2}}-1\right]\xi({\bf y^{\prime}})d{\bf y^{\prime}}\Bigg].

For
l\geq 2
, we decompose
|D|^{-1}A_{l}\xi
as

|D|^{-1}A_{l}\xi({\bf x^{\prime}})=-(1+e^{-2|D|})^{-1}\frac{1}{2\pi}\Big[\tilde{P}_{l}(\eta)\xi({\bf x^{\prime}})+\tilde{Q}_{l,*}(\eta)\xi({\bf x^{\prime}})+\tilde{R}_{l,*}(\eta)\xi({\bf x^{\prime}})\Big],

where the component operators are defined by

\displaystyle\tilde{P}_{l}(\eta)\xi({\bf x^{\prime}})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}}{|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}}\cdot\partial_{{\bf y^{\prime}}}\tilde{p}_{l}(P)\xi({\bf y^{\prime}})d{\bf y^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}_{l,*}(\eta)\xi({\bf x^{\prime}})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}}{|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}+4}\cdot(\partial_{{\bf y^{\prime}}}Q)\tilde{q}_{l-1}(Q,\kappa_{h})\xi({\bf y^{\prime}})d{\bf y^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\tilde{R}_{l,*}(\eta)\xi({\bf x^{\prime}})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Bigg(\frac{4Q}{(|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}+4)^{2}}\tilde{q}_{l-1}(Q,\kappa_{h})

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{2}{(|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}+4)^{3/2}}\tilde{q}_{l}(Q,\kappa_{h})\Bigg)\xi({\bf y^{\prime}})d{\bf y^{\prime}}.

The series expansions are given by

\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{\bf y^{\prime}}P}{(1+P^{2})^{3/2}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\bf y^{\prime}}\left(\frac{P}{(1+P^{2})^{1/2}}\right)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\geq 1\\
l\text{ odd}\end{subarray}}\partial_{\bf y^{\prime}}\tilde{p}_{l}(P),\quad\tilde{p}_{l}(P):=\tilde{a}_{l}P^{l},

\displaystyle\tilde{a}_{l}

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{1}{4}\right)^{(l-1)/2}\binom{l-1}{(l-1)/2},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1+\kappa_{h}Q+Q^{2})^{3/2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\tilde{q}_{l-1}(Q,\kappa_{h}),\quad\tilde{q}_{l-1}(Q,\kappa_{h}):=\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor(l-1)/2\rfloor}\tilde{b}_{l-1-j}\kappa_{h}^{l-1-2j}Q^{l-1},

\displaystyle\tilde{b}_{l-1-j}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{l-1-j}\frac{(2l-1-2j)!!}{2^{l-1-j}(l-1-j)!}\binom{l-1-j}{j}.

By Remark
3.1
, we focus on three integral operators in
A_{l}
. Applying Lemma
3.4
to
\tilde{P}_{l}
yields

\displaystyle\tilde{P}_{l}(\eta)\xi({\bf x^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}}{|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}}\cdot\tilde{p}_{l}(P)\partial_{{\bf y^{\prime}}}\xi({\bf y^{\prime}})d{\bf y^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}}{|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{3}}\cdot\tilde{a}_{l}P^{l-1}(\eta({\bf x^{\prime}})-\eta({\bf y^{\prime}}))\partial_{{\bf y^{\prime}}}\xi({\bf y^{\prime}})d{\bf y^{\prime}},

which decomposes into
\eta({\bf x^{\prime}})S_{l-1}\partial_{\bf x^{\prime}}\xi
and
S_{l-1}(\eta\partial_{\bf x^{\prime}}\xi)
. Applying Lemma
3.1
:

\displaystyle\|\tilde{P}_{l}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq\|\eta({\bf x^{\prime}})S_{l-1}\partial_{\bf x^{\prime}}\xi\|_{s,q}+\|S_{l-1}(\eta\partial_{\bf x^{\prime}}\xi)\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq Cl^{M+s}\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}^{l-2}\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\Big(\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}\|\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}\|\partial_{\bf x^{\prime}}\xi\|_{q_{2}}\Big).

For
\tilde{Q}_{l,*}
, we compute

\displaystyle\partial_{{\bf y^{\prime}}}Q

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial_{{\bf y^{\prime}}}\eta({\bf y^{\prime}})}{(|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}+4)^{1/2}}+\frac{(\eta({\bf x^{\prime}})+\eta({\bf y^{\prime}}))({\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}})}{(|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}+4)^{3/2}},

which gives

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}_{l,*}(\eta)\xi({\bf x^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}}{(|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}+4)^{3/2}}\cdot\partial_{{\bf y^{\prime}}}\eta({\bf y^{\prime}})\tilde{q}_{l-1}(Q,\kappa_{h})\xi({\bf y^{\prime}})d{\bf y^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}}{(|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}+4)^{5/2}}(\eta({\bf x^{\prime}})+\eta({\bf y^{\prime}}))\tilde{q}_{l-1}(Q,\kappa_{h})\xi({\bf y^{\prime}})d{\bf y^{\prime}}.

These correspond to
S_{l-1,*}(\partial_{\bf x^{\prime}}\eta\cdot\xi)
,
\eta({\bf x^{\prime}})S_{l-1,*}\xi
, and
S_{l-1,*}(\eta\xi)
. By Lemma
3.2
:

\displaystyle\|\tilde{Q}_{l,*}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C2^{l}\left(\|S_{l-1,*}(\partial_{\bf x^{\prime}}\eta\cdot\xi)\|_{s,q}+\|\eta({\bf x^{\prime}})S_{l-1,*}\xi\|_{s,q}+\|S_{l-1,*}(\eta\xi)\|_{s,q}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C2^{l}l^{s}\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}}^{l-2}\|\eta\|_{C^{s}}\Big(\|\partial_{\bf x^{\prime}}\eta\cdot\xi\|_{q}+\|\xi\|_{q_{2}}\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}+\|\eta\xi\|_{q}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C2^{l}l^{s}\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}}^{l-2}\|\eta\|_{C^{s}}\|\eta\|_{\max\{s,1\},q_{1}}\|\xi\|_{q_{2}}.

For
\tilde{R}_{l,*}
:

\displaystyle\tilde{R}_{l,*}(\eta)\xi({\bf x^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{4}{(|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}+4)^{5/2}}(\eta({\bf x^{\prime}})+\eta({\bf y^{\prime}}))\tilde{q}_{l-1}(Q,\kappa_{h})\xi({\bf y^{\prime}})d{\bf y^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor l/2\rfloor}\tilde{b}_{l-j}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{2}{(|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}+4)^{2}}\kappa_{h}^{l-2j}Q^{l-1}(\eta({\bf x^{\prime}})+\eta({\bf y^{\prime}}))\xi({\bf y^{\prime}})d{\bf y^{\prime}},

yielding
\eta({\bf x^{\prime}})S_{l-1,*}\xi
and
S_{l-1,*}(\eta\xi)
. Using Lemma
3.2
and
\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}}<1/4
:

\displaystyle\|\tilde{R}_{l,*}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C2^{l}\left(\|\eta({\bf x^{\prime}})S_{l-1,*}\xi\|_{s,q}+\|S_{l-1,*}(\eta\xi)\|_{s,q}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C2^{l}l^{s}\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}}^{l-2}\|\eta\|_{C^{s}}\|\xi\|_{q_{2}}\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}.

Combining estimates for
l\geq 2
:

\displaystyle\||D|^{-1}A_{l}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq\|\tilde{P}_{l}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}+\|\tilde{Q}_{l,*}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}+\|\tilde{R}_{l,*}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq Cl^{M+s}\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}^{l-2}\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\Big(\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}\|\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}\|\partial_{\bf x^{\prime}}\xi\|_{q_{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+C2^{l}l^{s}\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}}^{l-2}\|\eta\|_{C^{s}}\|\eta\|_{\max\{s,1\},q_{1}}\|\xi\|_{q_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C2^{l}l^{M+s}\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}^{l-2}\|\eta\|_{\max\{s,1\},q_{1}}\Big(\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}\|\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}\|\xi\|_{1,q_{2}}\Big).

For the remainder operator
A_{L}^{R}
:

\displaystyle\||D|^{-1}A_{L}^{R}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=L+1}^{\infty}\||D|^{-1}A_{l}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C2^{L}L^{M+s}\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}^{L-2}\|\eta\|_{\max\{s,1\},q_{1}}\Big(\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}\|\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}\|\xi\|_{1,q_{2}}\Big).

This completes the proof.
∎

During the proof of the preceding theorem, the following corollary is easily obtained.

Corollary 3.2
.

Let
\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{q_{1}}+\frac{1}{q_{2}}
with
\|\eta_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}},\|\eta_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}}<\frac{1}{4}
. For
\eta_{t}=\eta_{1}+t(\eta_{2}-\eta_{1})
where
t\in[0,1]
, the operators
A_{l}
and
A_{L}^{R}
satisfy

\displaystyle\left\||D|^{-1}\frac{d}{dt}A_{l}(\eta_{t})\xi\right\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C2^{l}l^{M+s+1}\|\eta_{t}\|_{C^{1}}^{l-2}\|\eta_{2}-\eta_{1}\|_{\max\{s,1\},q_{1}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\left(\|\eta_{t}\|_{C^{1}}\|\partial_{\mathbf{x^{\prime}}}\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta_{t}\|_{C^{s+1}}\|\xi\|_{1,q_{2}}\right),

and

\displaystyle\left\||D|^{-1}\frac{d}{dt}A_{L}^{R}(\eta_{t})\xi\right\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C2^{L}L^{M+s+1}\|\eta_{t}\|_{C^{1}}^{L-2}\|\eta_{2}-\eta_{1}\|_{\max\{s,1\},q_{1}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\left(\|\eta_{t}\|_{C^{1}}\|\partial_{\mathbf{x^{\prime}}}\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta_{t}\|_{C^{s+1}}\|\xi\|_{1,q_{2}}\right),

for all integers
l\geq 2
and
L\geq 2
, where
C>0
is a constant.

Proof.

For
l\geq 2
, we first analyze operator
A_{l}
:

|D|^{-1}\frac{d}{dt}A_{l}(\eta_{t})\xi({\bf x^{\prime}})=-(1+e^{-2|D|})^{-1}\frac{1}{2\pi}\Big[\frac{d}{dt}\tilde{P}_{l}(\eta_{t})\xi({\bf x^{\prime}})+\frac{d}{dt}\tilde{Q}_{l,*}(\eta_{t})\xi({\bf x^{\prime}})+\frac{d}{dt}\tilde{R}_{l,*}(\eta_{t})\xi({\bf x^{\prime}})\Big].

Analysis of
\frac{d}{dt}\tilde{P}_{l}(\eta_{t})
:

From Lemma
3.4
, we derive

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\tilde{P}_{l}(\eta_{t})\xi({\bf x^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=-\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}}{|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}}\cdot(\tilde{a}_{l}P^{l}(\eta_{t}))\partial_{{\bf y^{\prime}}}\xi({\bf y^{\prime}})d{\bf y^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}}{|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{3}}\cdot(l\tilde{a}_{l}P^{l-1}(\eta_{t}))(\eta_{2}-\eta_{1})\partial_{{\bf y^{\prime}}}\xi({\bf y^{\prime}})d{\bf y^{\prime}}.

Applying Lemma
3.1
yields

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{d}{dt}\tilde{P}_{l}(\eta_{t})\xi\right\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq Cl^{M+s+1}\|\eta_{t}\|_{C^{1}}^{l-2}\|\eta_{2}-\eta_{1}\|_{s,q_{1}}\Big(\|\eta_{t}\|_{C^{1}}\|\partial_{{\bf x^{\prime}}}\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta_{t}\|_{C^{s+1}}\|\partial_{\bf x^{\prime}}\xi\|_{q_{2}}\Big).

Analysis of
\frac{d}{dt}\tilde{Q}_{l,*}(\eta_{t})
:

We have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\tilde{Q}_{l,*}(\eta_{t})\xi({\bf x^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}}{(|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}+4)^{3/2}}\cdot\partial_{{\bf y^{\prime}}}(\eta_{2}-\eta_{1})({\bf y^{\prime}})\tilde{q}_{l-1}(Q,\kappa_{h})\xi({\bf y^{\prime}})d{\bf y^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad+(l-1)\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor(l-1)/2\rfloor}\tilde{b}_{l-1-j}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}}{(|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}+4)^{2}}\cdot\partial_{{\bf y^{\prime}}}\eta_{t}({\bf y^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Big[\kappa_{h}^{l-1-2j}Q^{l-2}\big((\eta_{2}-\eta_{1})({\bf x^{\prime}})+(\eta_{2}-\eta_{1})({\bf y^{\prime}})\big)\Big]\xi({\bf y^{\prime}})d{\bf y^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}}{(|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}+4)^{5/2}}\big((\eta_{2}-\eta_{1})({\bf x^{\prime}})+(\eta_{2}-\eta_{1})({\bf y^{\prime}})\big)\tilde{q}_{l-1}(Q,\kappa_{h})\xi({\bf y^{\prime}})d{\bf y^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad+(l-1)\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor(l-1)/2\rfloor}\tilde{b}_{l-1-j}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}}{(|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}+4)^{3}}\big(\eta_{t}({\bf x^{\prime}})+\eta_{t}({\bf y^{\prime}})\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Big[\kappa_{h}^{l-1-2j}Q^{l-2}\big((\eta_{2}-\eta_{1})({\bf x^{\prime}})+(\eta_{2}-\eta_{1})({\bf y^{\prime}})\big)\Big]\xi({\bf y^{\prime}})d{\bf y^{\prime}}.

Lemma
3.2
provides the bound

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{d}{dt}\tilde{Q}_{l,*}(\eta_{t})\xi\right\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C2^{l}l^{s+1}\|\eta_{t}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{l-2}\|\eta_{t}\|_{C^{s}}\|\eta_{2}-\eta_{1}\|_{\max\{s,1\},q_{1}}\|\xi\|_{q_{2}}.

Analysis of
\frac{d}{dt}\tilde{R}_{l,*}(\eta_{t})
:

Similarly,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\tilde{R}_{l,*}(\eta_{t})\xi({\bf x^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{4}{(|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}+4)^{5/2}}\big((\eta_{2}-\eta_{1})({\bf x^{\prime}})+(\eta_{2}-\eta_{1})({\bf y^{\prime}})\big)\tilde{q}_{l-1}(Q,\kappa_{h})\xi({\bf y^{\prime}})d{\bf y^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad+(l-1)\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor(l-1)/2\rfloor}\tilde{b}_{l-1-j}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{4}{(|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}+4)^{5/2}}(\eta_{t}({\bf x^{\prime}})+\eta_{t}({\bf y^{\prime}}))

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Big[\kappa_{h}^{l-1-2j}Q^{l-2}\big((\eta_{2}-\eta_{1})({\bf x^{\prime}})+(\eta_{2}-\eta_{1})({\bf y^{\prime}})\big)\Big]\xi({\bf y^{\prime}})d{\bf y^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad+l\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor l/2\rfloor}\tilde{b}_{l-j}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{2}{(|{\bf x^{\prime}}-{\bf y^{\prime}}|^{2}+4)^{2}}\kappa_{h}^{l-2j}Q^{l-1}\big((\eta_{2}-\eta_{1})({\bf x^{\prime}})+(\eta_{2}-\eta_{1})({\bf y^{\prime}})\big)\xi({\bf y^{\prime}})d{\bf y^{\prime}}.

Using Lemma
3.2
and the condition
\|\eta_{t}\|_{L^{\infty}}<1/4
:

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{d}{dt}\tilde{R}_{l,*}(\eta_{t})\xi\right\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C2^{l}l^{s+1}\|\eta_{t}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{l-2}\|\eta_{t}\|_{C^{s}}\|\xi\|_{q_{2}}\|\eta_{2}-\eta_{1}\|_{s,q_{1}}.

Combining these estimates yields the final bound for
A_{l}
. The estimate for
A_{L}^{R}
follows similarly.
∎

While estimates for the operator
A_{l}
are available in Craig
[
21
,
62
]
, we adapt these results to align with our nonlinear estimates and enhance their applicability.

Theorem 3.4
.

Let
\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{q_{1}}+\frac{1}{q_{2}}
. The operators
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
,
A_{l}
, and
A_{L}^{R}
satisfy the following estimates for some constant
C>0
:

\displaystyle\|A_{1}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\|\partial_{\mathbf{x^{\prime}}}\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\partial_{\mathbf{x^{\prime}}}\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{s,q_{1}}\|\partial_{\mathbf{x^{\prime}}}^{2}\xi\|_{s,q_{2}}\Big),

\displaystyle\|A_{2}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}\Big(\|\partial_{\mathbf{x^{\prime}}}\eta\|_{s+1,q_{1}}\|\xi\|_{s+1,q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{s+1,q_{1}}\|\partial_{\mathbf{x^{\prime}}}\xi\|_{s+1,q_{2}}\Big),

and for all integers
l\geq 3
,
L\geq 3
,

\displaystyle\|A_{l}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq Cl^{M+s+1}2^{l}\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}^{l-3}\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\times\Big(\|\partial_{\mathbf{x^{\prime}}}\eta\|_{s+1,q_{1}}\|\xi\|_{s+1,q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{s+1,q_{1}}\|\partial_{\mathbf{x^{\prime}}}\xi\|_{s+1,q_{2}}\Big),

\displaystyle\|A_{L}^{R}(\eta)\xi\|_{s,q}

\displaystyle\leq CL^{M+s+1}2^{L}\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}^{L-3}\|\eta\|_{C^{s+1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\times\Big(\|\partial_{\mathbf{x^{\prime}}}\eta\|_{s+1,q_{1}}\|\xi\|_{s+1,q_{2}}+\|\eta\|_{s+1,q_{1}}\|\partial_{\mathbf{x^{\prime}}}\xi\|_{s+1,q_{2}}\Big).
